    The smd6000 controller has DB9 connectors for the Analog and RS232 ports, which are active simultaneously with laser operation subject to various aspects of both.

    Analog port:

    The pinout of the Analog (Control) port DB9 is as follows: 

       Pin  Function
 ----------------------------------------------------------------------
    1   +5 VDC
    2   Interlock (connection 2) and Enable LED anode
    3   Reset switch (connection 1)
    4   Power Control (modulation) input (0 to 5 V, less than 30 kHz)
    5   GND
    6   Interlock (connection 1)
    7   Enable LED cathode
    8   Reset switch (connection 2)
    9   GND

    • Interlock: Attach an SPST switch between pins 6 and 2. If the laser is to be enabled all the time, this can be a wire, but see WARNINGs below.

    • Interlock/laser on LED: Attach an LED (no resistor) between pins 2 (anode) and 7 (cathode).

    • Reset: Attach a normally open pushbutton between pins 3 and 8. If the laser is to be enabled all the time, this can be a wire. But see WARNINGs below.

    • Power control: Attach a 10K ohm pot between pins 1 (CW, +5 VDC), 4 (wiper, Modulation input), and 5 (CCW, GND). Simply connect pin 1 to pin 4 for full power. This can also be a TTL (on/off, with pullup) or analog (continuously variable) signal between pins 4 and 5, which can be used to modulate the laser at up to 30 kHz. The input is internally clamped at 5 V so it can't be accidentally overdriven, at least not easily.

    The LED will be illuminated once the interlock conditions are satisfied AND the laser had been turned on via the firmware. The LED should be taken seriously. It means that the laser may be ready to output full power without warning. There is no other emission indicator.

    WARNING: If the laser was outputting a beam and the Interlock connection is broken, the laser will turn off. If the interlock (and Reset if needed) is re-established, the beam will appear at the previous power with no delay.

    WARNING: Should the laser shut down due to a fault like excessive temperature, with Interlock and Reset completed, the beam may reappear suddenly if the fault condition is resolved. Therefore, implementing Reset as a momentary switch is highly recommended.

    RS232 Port:

    RS232 communications allows the laser to be turned on and off, and selected parameters to be changed or monitored. For the end-user version, the RS232 is only required to change default settings, not to turn the laser on as that can be done totally from the Control port. However, some OEM versions require RS232 to enable the output even if it's desired to return to a default state. There is no way to power up the laser without issuing at least 2 RS232 commands. I've heard there may be a way around this (I'm suspecting an internal jumper) but haven't found it yet.

    I may be able to provide a dongle that plugs into the RS232 and Control connectors allowing for limited control of Laser Quantum lasers. It is based on an Atmel microcontroller and has a button, a few LEDs, and a trim-pot to adjust power, as well as external interlock and modulation inputs. But it's presently somewhat brain-dead as far as flexibility is concerned. This version simply initializes the laser to 500 mW, enables the output, and allows the on-board trim-pot to reduce power. However, the source code is available so some hacking is certainly possible. If interested, please contact me via the Sci.Electronics.Repair FAQ Email Links Page.

    Only pins 2, 3, and 5 are used on the RS232 DB9. The required cable is the normal straight through, DB9F to DB9M. The serial port runs at 9600 baud, no parity, and 1 stop bit. There is no hand shaking. The user must wait for the laser to respond to a command before issuing another. The terminal emulator should be set up to echo (half duplex) and do automatic LF after CR. Commands are case-insensitive and terminate with CR. (For information on using RS232, see the section: RS232 Control of Solid State Lasers.)

    However, Laser Quantum has a Windows application that will run these lasers via RS232 or the Internet. Go to "Products", "Options and Custimisaton", "RemoteApp Laser Control", or direct from the Laser Quantum RemoteApp Laser Control Software Page.

    With RemoteApp, there is no need to worry about remmembering obscure commands or setting up terminal options for basic operation of the laser. However, it may not be able to change settings like the startup mode or set output power calibration (at least not without some magic password), so raw RS232 may be needed for those. There are only a few commands that are needed to control the laser and monitor its status. Some of these are NOT valid with the OEM version of these lasers. In the table below, the "?" are part of those query commands that have them and are required. "XXXX" can be 1 to 4 decimal digits. 

       Command      Type   Description
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ON       Action  If Interlock and Reset are closed the laser will turn
                        on (opens shutter) Enable LED will turn on.

                       If Interlock is closed but not Reset, the laser will be
                        "armed" so that closing Reset will turn it and
                        the Enable LED on.

     OFF       Action  Turns laser off (closes shutter).  Enable LED will
                        turn off.  This occurs regardless of whether Reset
                        is closed or not.  (The laser can also be turned off
                        by interrupting Interlock.)

    STAT?      Query   Returns on/off status of laser (but NOT the states of
                        Interlock and Reset).

    POWER?     Query   Returns internally sensed laser power in mW.  This
                        is calibrated relative to the ACTP command (below).

   CURRENT?    Query   Returns pump diode current in percent relative to
                        current limit.  The Power Control input (pot or signal)
                        on the Analog port must be at maximum (5 V) for
                        this value to make sense.  No one seems to know why
                        the power input matters.

  POWER=XXXX   Action  Sets laser power in mW.  The firmware then immediately
                        ramps up or down to the new power level.  This can
                        take a minute or more depending on how much the new
                        power differs from the original.

                        If XXXX=0, the laser switches to constant current
                        mode at the present diode current.

  STEN=YES/NO  Action  Enables (YES) or disables (NO) laser turning on at
                        startup without requiring RS232 commands.  (Not on
                        OEM version.)  Must be followed by a WRITE command.

   STPOW=XXXX  Action  Sets startup output power.  (Not on OEM version.)
                        Must be followed by a WRITE command.

   ACTP=XXXX   Action  Laser power calibration.  The value entered is assumed
                        to be the (externally) measured laser power.  The
                        internal calibration is adjusted accordingly and the
                        actual power is changed to what was set in the
                        last POWER=XXXX command or default.  Must be followed
                        by a WRITE command.  (May not be valid on all versions
                        of firmware.)

    WRITE      Action  Write user data to non-volatile memory.  This is
                        required after changing a value to save it after
                        power is removed.  (On the OEM version, the only
                        value where this seems to matter is the ACTP
                        calibration.)

   LASTEMP?    Query   Returns the laser head temperature in °C.

   PSUTEMP?    Query   Returns the power supply/controller temperature in °C.

    TIMERS     Query   Returns the accumulated hours for the laser head,
                        controller, and how long the pump diode has been
                        running at greater than 1 A (which is usually almost
                        identical to the laser head hours).

    The commands must be typed exactly as they appear above (though the parser is case-insensitive). There don't appear to be abbreviations that work. If a command isn't recognized, an error will be generated with a decimal value related to the ASCII code of the first character. Or something. :)

    For the end-user version of the GEM and IGNIS, it is possible to issue the STEN and STPOW commands so that the RS232 port is not needed to start the laser. However, this is not possible with the OEM GEM and IGNIS lasers now appearing surplus, as they have special firmware to disable these features (presumably for safety reasons).

    There are additional variations on some of these commands. See the operation manual for details. :)

    In addition to its intended use for calibration, the ACTP command can fake out the controller to obtain higher output power than would otherwise be possible. Although values larger than the rated output power can be entered via the "POWER=XXX" command, there appears to be a limit. For example, entering more than 700 or so mW for the 500 mW laser will have no effect. However, if set for 500 mW and outputting 500 mW, entering "ACTP=300 mW" will cause the output power to increase to over 800 mW if the laser is capable of it. The diode current won't go above 100% under any circumstances, but there may also be an absolute power limit since some of these lasers will not ever output more than some maximum power even at well below 100% diode current.

    CAUTION I do not know if it is safe or recommended for the laser to run at a power level like 1.2 W that is so far above the rated value. Even with excellent cooling, it may be stressful for the diode, crystals, and optics.

    Quick start guide:

    With the OEM lasers, one procedure for turning the laser on is to power up the controller, close the Interlock, issue the "ON" command, press Reset, and issue the "POWER=XXX" command. The output power will then ramp up slowly to the selected power. If Interlock and Reset are hardwired, then it's just a matter of issuing the two RS232 commands. Dust off that pre-Jurassic laptop. :) Or, if you're handy with 0s and 1s, program a PIC to do it.

    General comments on stability:

    These lasers are not designed for the highest stability. The specification is 0.8 percent RMS or 2 percent p-p. Some of the lasers I've tested were worse (though that may be why they became available). However, the percent variation depends on the output power and seems to be better at higher power.

    Where a non-zero power level has been specified via the RS232 port (POWER=XXX), the laser will operate in constant power mode attempting to maintain the selected power by controlling diode current. The output power then tends to jump around by more than one would expect based on the specifications. Perhaps the resolution of the current driver D/A in the digital control loop for active power control is a bit marginal. But if a POWER=0 command is issued, it switches to constant current mode at the present diode current. The modulation signal/pot on the Analog port still has an effect, but there is no longer any active feedback to regulate power. This actually results in much better short term stability. There is still the normal mode sweep, which for one of the lasers I've tested was several percent p-p. But after the laser has warmed up for 1/2 hour or more, power variation over a few minutes was less than 0.2 percent. For another laser, it was under 0.1 percent. With a temperature-controlled baseplate, it should be even better.

    (From: Kevin Criqui.)

    There is (at least) one more command that's fairly useful when tuning these up. DUMP_ where the "_" is actually a space dumps out a bunch of system parameters (same as shown at the bottom of the diagnostic report in the GUI). DUMP w/o the space gives an error. Sneaky buggers!

    Laser Quantum GEM 532 and IGNIS 660 Laser Head

    The interior of a GEM laser head is shown in Laser Quantum GEM 532 Cavity Components and Output Optics. (The organization of the IGNIS 660 cavity is essentially identical.) Both the GEM and IGNIS use a miniature L-fold cavity design where a 45 degree mirror coated HT@532 and HR@1064 (or HT@660 and HR@1320 for the IGNIS 660) acts as the OC. This is more efficient than the common linear cavity since virtually all the green light ends up in the output beam, and none passes through the lasing crystal. The total (optical) distance between mirrors is around 5 cm. A C-mount pump diode goes through a GRIN lens, cylindrical lens, and focusing lens, passes through the front HR mirror to the Vanadate crystal, which is enclosed in a copper block (probably with indium foil) for heat dissipation. The KTP is enclosed in an aluminum block so it can be temperature controlled. There are TECs for the laser diode and KTP. The entire cavity is on a separate plate, so it could also be temperature controlled, but I don't believe that is implemented in this laser.

    After the output beam passes through the 45 degree OC, it bounces off a turn mirror, goes through a lens and beam sampler (beam splitter) for output power monitoring, then another plate or lens, and finally to the focusing lens to couple it into the multi-mode fiber. On lasers with free-space output, there is only a window.

    See Interior of GEM 532 Laser Head in All its Glory. for a laser running at about 100 mW.

    Most of the cavity optics are on mounts that in principle have enough degrees of freedom to allow alignment. Of course, there are no 100 tpi knobs, only screws securing everything to the baseplate with most of the optics themselves glued onto the mounts. Therefore, without factory jigs, it's somewhat hit or miss attempting to adjust some of the critical optics. For example, tightening the single screw securing the SHG crystal/TEC tends to change alignment. Sort of like in golf estimating a putt on a sloping green. But others like the mount for the lasing crystal (Vanadate, YAG, or YLF) uses a pair of screws and is better behaved. (I would not recommend touching the alignment of any of the cavity mirrors unless absolutely necessary.) The factory also seems to have had a problem with using a mixture of cap head hex and security Torx screws, and even a few slotted heads thrown in for good measure. :) Perhaps there's a method to the madness, but it's certainly not obvious! However, with care, it is possible to restore a laser with a poor beam profile and low power to a usable state if alignment was the problem, though achieving spec or better performance may be more of challenge. To summarize:

    • The nice thing about these lasers is that almost everything that may need adjustment is secured by screws.

    • The horrible thing about these lasers is that almost everything that may need adjustment is secured by screws.

    Having said that, it has been possible to bring both GEM and IGNIS lasers back from the dead:

    • A GEM originally had a totally splotchy beam and produced no more than 10 mW. By realigning the SHG, this laser now will now run at up to more than 200 mW continuously. The beam is still not pretty suggesting that one of the intra-cavity optics is damaged, but the central area appears to be TEM00, and output from the fiber would be fine. 200 mW is way below the spec power of 500 or 600 mW, but still useful.

    • One IGNIS originally had a TEM01 beam and ran at only about 20 mW max. Realigning the laser crystal mount enabled it to produce over 300 mW continuously. That actually meets specifications, though it's probably below where it was when new. Additional power might be obtained if an XYP micropositioner were used rather than bare hands. :)

    • Another IGNIS originally produced a maximum 20 mW. Repositioning the vanadate crystal, KTP, and aperture plate, and adjusting the HR alignment (pitch) enabled it to produce over 200 mW in a perfect beam. This is still below spec but it appears stable and would be quite usable.

    • Other similar lasers have been greatly improved through a combination of electronic adjustments (LD and KTP temperature) and alignment. Some have been quite dramatic, increasing output power by more than a factor of 4 or more even if the beam profile was decent (by eye at least). Sometimes, the electronic adjustments alone were sufficient to achieve spec power or better, but cavity alignment seems to drift on many of these lasers, and I doubt they were all dropped or whacked with a hammer!

    In all cases, adjustments to the optics were done at very low power so that the cavity geometry alone would control the modes, not thermal lensing. The "POWER=0" command was issued to lock the diode current at a constant value. After the output power was peaked with the best beam profile, the current was increased to 100 percent. The diode and SHG temperature settings in the controller were then adjusted for maximum power.

    Exactly how these lasers lost alignment in the first place is not clear. The screws were *very* tight so it's unlikely to have happened due to physical shock. Any blow that would have shifted alignment would have likely left tell-tail signs of trauma either on the outside of the case or in the form of pieces of broken optics. It's possible that the optics had damaged coatings or contamination in some cases rather than being misaligned, but nothing was obvious. Not surprisingly, older and/or higher mileage lasers have tended to have more severe issues of this type, but not always.

    However, another head had a fault that would be very difficult to repair: The TEC for the pump diode was open, so its temperature increases until the laser shuts down with a "D1 Overtemp" fault (red LED on). Once the pump diode has cooled sufficiently, it turns back on. So, if Interlock and Reset were both closed, the laser will cycle on and off at a rate dependent on the temperature. This is not exactly a safe situation with a relatively high power laser coming on without warning! For example, at 300 mW, it will be on for about 45 seconds and off for about 15 seconds on a fan-cooled heatsink. At 50 mW, it may stay on continuously. However, with the dead TEC, the pump diode won't be operating at the temperature for optimal absorption in the lasing crystal and will likely be running at a higher current than it would be with proper cooling. The failure has been confirmed to be inside the TEC, which would require removal of the entire pump assembly to replace.

    While the GEM 532 product specifications found on-line list 100:1 linear polarization for the output, some of the OEM versions have a "homogenizer" optic inside at the output to scramble the polarization, presumably to reduce instability when coupling into a multimode fiber. Without the fiber, the free-space beam appears perfect TEM00 (at least by eye). However, when a polarizing filter is placed in front of the laser and oriented horizontal or vertical, the appearance looks like interference fringe patterns as shown in Strange Polarization of Some OEM GEM 532 Green DPSS Lasers. When I first saw this, I thought it was a problem with the specific laser. But that laser was in fact super healthy as far as output power goes - capable of over 1.4 WATTs with a beautiful TEM00 beam, an unlikely candidate for there to be a problem. It never even occurred to be to test polarization on these lasers until someone else commented that an LQ GEM laser they had acquired seemed to be non-polarized. A representative of from Laser Quantum said all their lasers come with linear polarization and suggested that something may have fractured. At first, this seemed to make sense, of sorts. I then tested another GEM 532 and it was almost pure linearly polarized, with just a hint of what looked like a very weak higher order spatial mode or modes in the orthogonal polarization, but probably met the 100:1 spec. So perhaps tech support was correct after all. But since the total beam profile (on the weird laser) is so good, any failure must be outside the laser cavity, leaving the optics at the lower right in Laser Quantum GEM 532 Cavity Components and Output Optics. Anything inside the cavity would almost certainly result in a messed up beam profile accompanied by a serious reduction in output power - if it would lase at all. And the pattern remains absolutely stable from lasing threshold to over 1 W. It was thus highly unlikely to be the result of higher order spatial modes or something like that, which would almost certainly change in some way as power is increased. But I kept thinking how nice and uniform the stripes are and almost perfectly aligned horizontally. This must be a feature, not a bug. :) After removing the cover on the laser that was linearly polarized, it was confirmed that the component called "Homogenizer Plate" is missing in that laser as shown in Interior of Linearly Polarized Laser Quantum GEM 532 Green DPSS Laser. On another GEM 532 with stripes, the plate is present. Upon testing, the beam before the plate is linearly polarized while after it the beam shows no polarization preference (the stripes are simply too small to resolve easily at that location). The "Homogenizer Plate" is probably a wedged birefringent material so that it changed waveplate behavior in a periodic manner vertically over the extent of the beam.

    I have confirmed that simply removing the homogenizer plate (by breaking it free with padded tweezers or something more drastic) results in a nice linearly polarized beam with just the slightest detectable higher order modes in the orthogonal polarization (similar to the laser that never had the plate). Since the thick optic was angled to avoid direct back-reflections, the beam pointing alignment changed by a small amount and the beam exits the laser at a slightly different angle. However, since these lasers were intended to be fiber-coupled, the exit angle is not a critical specification and was not really very close to 0 degrees on any of the lasers I've tested. With a slight adjustment of the fiber connector position, it still couples efficiently into a fiber (though the stability may be reduced). Of course, the entire purpose of the optic-ectomy was to have a linearly polarized free-space beam! :)

    Another side effect of the birefringence of the Homogenizer Plate is that if the output of the laser is collimated by adding an external lens, the result isn't the expected and desired single beam, but two beams having orthogonal polarization that are separated vertically by about 1 mR. So, the homogenizer plate behaves like a wimpy Wallaston prism. "It's a feature, not a bug.". :) Regardless, when using one of these for an application like a laser show projector, removal of the plate would also be required to produce a single beam (in addition to the need for linear polarization by an external AOM or PCAOM).

    Here is the procedure in detail. Ideally, the following should be done in a cleanroom or at least a glove box. However, a reasonably clean dust-free area will suffice, or better yet inside a clean plastic bag. (Put some anti-static foam over the electrical connector to avoid the possibility of ESD daamge.) Also, have a piece of glass or clear plastic cut approximately to the size of the laser head to serve as a temporary cover.

    WARNING: Potentially hazardous stray beam(s) may be present when the laser head cover is removed.

    CAUTION: Doing this voids the warranty. :)

    1. Slit the two warranty seals with a knife or razor blade. Use a security Torx driver to remove the 6 screws fastening the head cover but do not remove the cover just yet.

    2. Run the laser at low power (e.g., power=25). Once it reaches the selected power, send the "power=0" command to put it in constant current mode. Then Turn the beam off by issuing the "off" command, disabling the interlock, or turning down the power adjust pot.

    3. Use a thin blade to remove the head cover taking care that the O-ring remains in its groove on the bottom part. Place your transparent cover in place to enable examination of the interior. (And to take photos for posterity!) When not accessing the interior, leave the transparent lid in place.

    Refer to Laser Quantum GEM 532 Cavity Components and Output Optics for the following:

    1. Use the appropriate hex driver to remove the screw securing the assembly with the Beam Sampler and Homogenizer Plate and carefully lift it free. CAUTION: Avoid touching the beam sampler plate!!! Completely removing the assembly rather than attempting to work on it in-place avoids the possibility of polarization scrambler plate shards getting into the laser head interior.

    2. Use a razor blade or knife to carefully cut through the glue securing the Output (homogenizer) Plate and then a pair of padded tweezers to break it free. The adhesive is hard, so it may simply be more expedient to break the plate free with a pair of pliers! The plate material is very fragile and may crumble. Clear out the debris and dust off the beam sampler assembly (now devoid of the Homogenizer Plate) to make sure none of the debris gets back into the laser head.

    3. Replace the assembly (now with only the Beam Sampler) but do not tighten the screw just yet.

    4. Turn the beam back on by issuing the "on" command, restoring the interlock, or turning up the pot.

    5. Adjust the angle of the Beam Sampler so that only one of the two spots hits the monitor photodiode with the other one being blocked as shown in Interior of GEM 532 Laser Head in All its Glory. Tighten the screws and confirm that the orientation hasn't changed.

    6. Inspect the output beam profile. It should be essentially unchanged by eye but with a polarizer, should be greater than 100:1 horizontally polarized. If there are any unsightly blemishes, check for debris on the Fiber Coupler (lens), beam sampler plate, or elsewhere.

    7. Replace the cover taking care that the O-ring remains in its groove and tighten the 6 screws incrementally in an alternating pattern.

    8. Run the laser up to full power and confirm beam quality and polarization.

    The procedure may be simplified by removing the Homogenizer Plate in-place. This eliminates the need to run in constant current mode or alignment of the beam sampler, but there is a greater risk of leaving debris inside the laser head.

    Where there is a desire to possibly still run the laser with a fiber, the position of the FC connector will probably need to be adjusted slightly due to the change in angle of the output beam. Attach a fiber and loosen the three screws securing the FC connector so it is just snug. Adjust its position for maximum power out of the fiber. Tighten the screws incrementally and confirm that the coupling efficiency hasn't changed. Note that the stability of the output through the fiber may be compromised by removing the homogenizer plate.

    smd6000 DPSS Laser Controller

    The same controller is used for the GEM, IGNIS, LUX, and others. There are at least two versions of the smd6000. The older version (pre around 2012) are most common. I expected it to be microprocessor controlled with no analog adjustments. While it does use a microprocessor, as can be seen in Interior of Laser Quantum smd6000 DPSS Laser Controller 1, there are ten (10), count 'em, trimpots! I believe the single QFP on the PCB *is* an 8051. There are six LM2679s - National Semiconductor's SIMPLE SWITCHER® 5 A Step-Down Voltage Regulator with Adjustable Current Limit. I don't know if these are simply to provide the needed DC voltages or are the actual current drivers for the pump diode and TECs (or a combination). Five of the trimpots are covered in red Loctite™, presumably because one isn't supposed to adjust those for fear of blowing up the Universe (or at least the laser). But the other five are not, and two of them do seem to be useful being the temperature set-points for the pump diode and SHG crystal. One or two of the others probably are related to output power calibration or range. None are actually labeled with their function but the temperature adjustments are near test connectors with labels for Diode 1, KTP, and Cavity. (These lasers do not have a cavity TEC, so that trimpot doesn't do much.)

    Interestingly, the test-points and LEDs do have labels, so it may not be totally impossible to figure out the functions of most of the adjustments based on their proximity to test-points and effect on operation. But it's still probably not a good idea to fiddle with the 5 sealed trimpots and risk blowing up the Universe. :)

    I thought that the green LEDs labeled D1 Pelt, D2 Pelt, KTP Pelt, and Cav Pelt should be on when their respective temperatures are properly regulated. But their behavior would tend to be otherwise. KTP Pelt may be off even when the KTP temperature is obviously stable and optimal (as in the photo). And D1 Pelt seems to be off with the pot turned beyond a specific point clockwise. There is only a single pump diode so D2 Pelt is always off. And why does -5V qualify for it's own LED, but no other power supply voltages? :)

    The laser which has been immortalized in the portraits originally only produced a maximum of about 10 mW in a splotchy beam. Fiddling with the positions of the mounts for the Vanadate and KTP resulted in more than 200 mW with a decent central beam profile, though there is still significant scatter above and below. However, the profile remains about the same down to the lasing threshold, so I believe that alignment is not the problem limiting output power, but as noted above, manual alignment is almost impossible, so it may not be quite optimal. So, while I don't believe the original problem was alignment though that can't be ruled out. The KTP mount in particular, which is relatively massive as these things go, is only fastened with a single screw at one end, so a sharp blow from the side of the laser head could in principle change its position. However, the screw was quite tight, so any blow would had to be extremely severe and likely broken something or left a tell-tail mark on the case.

    The LD and KTP temperatures were also adjusted via the controller trim-pots. The KTP Pelt LED tends to flicker but is often off more than it is on, so I'm not totally sure that's working correctly in this particular laser. (In the photo of the controller, above, it's off.) And the D1 Pelt LED doesn't quite work as expected either in that there seems to be a point clockwise where it will always be off, still well within the expected useful range, and the setting for optimum pump diode wavelength may be more clockwise. Also, the point where the LED goes off does not move even when the diode temperature changes. Going too far counterclockwise results in the output power declining sharply and the beam profile blossoming, and much of the power never gets out of the laser. So that direction - which is probably increasing the diode temperature - does work. The key to success in adjusting the trim-pots is to be patient. The time constants are very long - 30 seconds to minutes. So try a fraction of a turn and wait for a response. 1/10th of a turn is significant, especially for KTP Pelt, and the response of the output is periodic. So, rotating in one direction will result in a series of peaks with the desired one being the maximum of the peaks, in the center of an envelope with a broad maximum. Finally, note that the optimum settings depend on pump power so both trim-pots should be optimized at the expected operating point.

    As noted above, many if not most of the OEM smd6000 controllers are set up to require RS232 commands turn on the laser after power is applied and the STEN firmware command is disabled. However, a few apparently identical lasers have not been made brain dead in this manner and behave like the end-user manual states. I was hoping this was a matter of a jumper setting. Interior of Laser Quantum smd6000 DPSS Laser Controller 2 is a photo of the interior of one such unit. I can find no differences. And Laser Quantum has confirmed that this is either due to different firmware (SMD-G-1.1.2 rather than SMD-G-1.1.1) or more likely, a password protected or hidden command.

    As of 2012, there is a totally redesigned smd6000 which looks identical on the outside, but has a much smaller PCB with fewer components and only a hand-full of trim-pots that don't appear to be particularly useful for tuning up a laser. See Interior of Laser Quantum smd6000 DPSS Laser Controller 3. Perhaps LQ got annoyed that I was telling people how to adjust these lasers to optimize performance! Gone are trim-pots for any of the temperature settings. So, now it's all done by undocumented firmware commands!

    smd12 DPSS Laser Controller

    The smd12 is the replacement for the smd6000 controller. It is much smaller and lighter but with largely the same capabilities. However, there are some subtle changes mostly relating to the Control and RS232 interface. The SMD12 has only a single DB9F connector for both so the RS232 pinout is non-standard and apparently a closely guarded trade secret by Novanta (the current name or replacement for Laser Quantum). And to top it off, the manuals found on line have major errors in the tables and text on pages 11-13, though the diagrams are correct and following them will result in a functioning laser IFF it has been set to power up without requiring any RS232 commands. But never fear, here is the needed info. ;-)

    Control Port: 

     Pin  Function
------------------------------------------------------------------
  1   +5 VDC
  2   RS232 TXD
  3   Enable Switch Connection 1
  4   Jumper to Pin 1.  See below.
  5   Interlock Connection 2, Enable LED anode, and Ground (GND)
  6   Interlock Connection 1
  7   Enable LED Cathode
  8   Enable Switch Connection 2
  9   RS232 RXD

    Normal setup:

    • Jumper Pin 1 to Pin 4.

    • Interlock: Pin 6 to Pin 5. Use an SPST switch if desired to be able to shut the laser with the switch. However, this can be done trivially (without RS232) by pulling the DB9 or killing power. Otherwise it can be a jumper, or an actual interlock chain. Gasp. ;-)

    • Laser ON LED: Connect to Pin 5 (Anode) and Pin 7 (Cathode). Most common LEDs are satisfactory as the current is less than 20 mA. However, modern high brightness LEDs (usually green, blue, or white) will be blindingly bright!

    • Enable: Pin 3 to Pin 8. If this is an SPST momentary switch, pressing it will turn on the laser if it isn't OFF in the RS232. If it is an SPST normal switch and in the ON position, the laser will turn on as long as the RS232 status is NOT set to OFF. THIS INCLUDES AT POWER UP!

    • RS232 (Optional) Pins 2 (TXD), Pin 9 (RXD), Pin 5 (GND).

    Or in more detail and to rephrase:

    Enable Switch: Closing this switch will turn on the laser if the interlocks are enabled. If it is closed (along with the interlocks) when DC power is applied, the laser will turn on at the set power if it is set to do that using the STEN commend. Opening this switch has no immediate effect but would prevent the RS232 from turning the laser back on after it being off.

    RS232: Pins 2, 9, and 5. 9600 Baud, one stop bit, no parity, no handshaking, half duplex. Backspace does the correct thing, more-or-less. RS232 is only needed to change power. The laser can be set to come up with the default power set by the STPOW command as described above.

    The POWER? command reads the actual power determined by internal sensor (subject to calibration).

    The POWER=xxx sets the power but takes awhile to ramp up or down.

    The STPOW=xxx command has no immediate effect. But if the WRITE command is issued afterwards, it is saved and will be applied when the laser comes up after being power-cycled.

    Using a potentiometer to control power via pin 4 (using +5 Pin 1 and GND Pin 5) may be possible but the laser I tested was shutting down at random so it is not known if this really works or is even safe for the laser.

    The Timers on the laser I tested do not seem to change. :-(

    When the Enable button or switch is closed AND the internal state determined by the RS232 is ON, the laser will begin its power up sequence, which is downright boring compared to some other lasers. ;-) It will ramp up the pump current until the STPOW value is reached. From Enable to first laser light may take awhile.

    And for the higher power lasers, make sure there is a proper beam stop! Originally, I didn't realize the laser would come on at full power, so while fiddling around attempting to get RS232 going, the laser was happily ramping up power and then burning a hole in a piece of white-painted wood. I now use a real red brick. These make decent beam stops for up to at least dozens of watts. Their surfaces reflect little light and it's diffuse, and they can take a lot of punishment.


  • Back to Commercial Solid State Lasers Sub-Table of Contents.

    B&W Tek 532-10-OEM 10 mW DPSS Laser

    B&W Tek is a manufacturer/supplier of a variety of lasers, laser systems, Raman spectrometers, and other photonics-related equipment. My first contact with B&W Tek was when we bought a fiber-coupled 980 nm 1 W laser diode from them for research at Drexel U. Later, I found a driver PCB of theirs inside The Edmund Scientific Model L54-101 Green Laser Pointer. And lasers similar to the ones described below are commonly available on eBay.

    The following applies to other B&W Tek lasers using similar controllers.

    The 532-10-OEM is a cute little DPSS green laser system consisting of a laser head and compact controller that runs on 5 VDC as shown in B&W Tek 532-10-OEM Green DPSS Laser System. The laser head has a TEC for the pump diode and crystal assembly. The laser runs in light feedback. After a short warmup period as the temperature reaches the set-point, the output settles down and is very stable. These lasers are TEM00 but are not spec'd to be single frequency, though I haven't actually tested them in this regard.

    Discrete Cavity of Typical B&W Tek Green DPSS Laser shows what's inside the laser head one sample. (This is not the same model but should be similar.) A C-mount-style pump diode is butt-coupled to a piece of Vanadate (visible attached to a copper plate). The KTP is in the center with the OC on the right. For size reference, the KTP is probably 2x2x5 mm. It's not clear how cavity alignment is achieved in the vertical direction. Since there is only a single TEC for the entire cavity, the optimal temperature will be a tradeoff between KTP phase matching and pump diode absorption. Presumably, the temperature is set via the TEC so the pump diode wavelength optimal for peak absorption and the KTP is then angle tuned for maximum output. The TEC can them fine tune the KTP phase matching without changing the diode wavelength very much.

    There are at least two different versions of the controller which I assume are equivalent, though that hasn't been confirmed. However, the layout inside is very similar and both have 5 unmarked pots as shown in B&W Tek DPSS Laser Controller (Older Version) and B&W Tek DPSS Laser Controller (Newer Version). The trim-pots are as follows (for both versions of the controller): 

        Pot ID (Older)    Pot ID (Newer)    Function
  ----------------------------------------------------------------------
         R14               W1           Pump Diode Current Limit
         R13               W2           Current Control
         R20               W3           Light Feedback Calibration
         R40               W4           Diode Temerature
         R41               W5           SHG Temperature

    Unless a new pump diode is installed, R14/W1 should NOT be changed as there is risk of blowing the diode if it is set too high, even for a microsecond. It is likely to have sealer to let you know. :) R13/W2 will adjust diode current up to the current limit if running in Constant Current (CC) mode with no light feedback or insufficient light feedback. R20/W3 will adjust the output power (subject to the settings of R14/R13/W1/W2) in Constant Power (CP) mode where there is a beam sampler to sense the output power AND if there is sufficient output power. Where the laser has two TECs, R40/W4 sets the temperature of the diode and R41/W5 sets the temperature of the SHG crystal (doubler, KTP for 532 nm; LBO or BBO for 474 nm). Where the laser has a single TEC, only one of these two trim-pots may be present. R41/W5 in the laser I checked.

    Controller power connector (DB9M) pinout: 

         Pin      Function
   ----------------------------------------------------------------------
      1       GND
      2       GND
      3       TTL Modulation/Enable.  Float/high is on.
      4       +5 VDC
      5       +5 VDC
      6       Interlock, jumpered to 7.
      7       Interlock, jumpered to 6.
      8       Analog Modulation.
      9       NC

    Most of these lasers that I've seen only require +5 VDC, GND, and Interlock jumpered between pins 6 and 7. However, it appears as though some, perhaps higher power lasers, require 6 and 7 to also be jumpered to GND.

    On the unit I tested, Analog Modulation (or power control) can either be a voltage or simply a resistor to GND. 0 to 10K ohms provided the full range from 0 to max output. A voltage from 0 to a few V positive did the same.

    Controller laser head connector (DB9F) pinout: 

         Pin      Function
   ----------------------------------------------
      1       Light Feedback Photodiode Anode
      2       10K Thermistor Temperature Sensor
      3       TEC-
      4       Pump Diode Cathode
      5       TEC+
      6       ???
      7       Light Feedback Photodiode Cathode
      8       Pump Diode Anode
      9       10K Thermistor Temperature Sensor

    There is a silicon diode across the pump diode for reverse polarity protection.

    With it simple adjustments and no digital magic, these controllers would be very well suited for general purpose use driving other small single-TEC DPSS laser heads.

    Some of these lasers show up with a second PCB piggy-backed on top of the main one. This appears to be a digital controller most likely to enable remote setting and monitoring of the laser parameters. On the 473 nm laser I have like this, there is no output beam if the second PCB is removed. On another laser that was faulty, there was a weak beam and behavior was similar regardless of whether the digital PCB was present. When the top board is present, 0 ohm jumpers R54 and R55 next to trim-pots W4 and W5, respectfully, have been removed. So this probably transfers control to the digital controller. Installing jumpers for R54 and R55 should allow the analog controller to run without the piggyback board, though this has not as yet been confirmed. CAUTION: Since W4 and W5 may not be used when the digital controller is present, their settings may be far off.

    Might laser runs with light feedback as confirmed by removing the beam sampler. With it in place, output power is around 6 mW and stable; with it removed, power peaks at well over 25 mW and varies with mode sweep. And contrary to what is claimed on at least one Web forum, the LEDs do not change status when it's regulating properly.

    Here are closeup photos (courtesy of Dave Tucker) of the two PCBs:

    • Closeup of B&W Tek Analog Control PCB LDTEC-12 VER. 5.0 02/04. In this case, the W2 trim-pot was replaced with wires to be installed externally somewhere somehow.

    • Closeup of B&W Tek Digital Control PCB MIC CON VER 4.0 09/03. And as confirmation, "MIC" likely stands for "Microprocessor". :)

    And, apparently, the digital controller is quite sophisticated, capable of optimizing lasing parameters as well as having not 1, but 2 RS232 ports. A discussion may be found on the B&W TEk Thread on Photonlexicon. Newer versions of the digital PCB include labels for the various connectors. What a concept! See BWTEK Control Board Top. Here is a summary: 

      Connector   # Pins  Function    Comments
-----------------------------------------------------------
     J1          3    I-P PI, Pd  Connects to Analog PCB
     J2          2    Rt1, Rt2    Connects to Analog PCB
     J3          6    Vcon1-2     Connects to Analog PCB
     J4          3    CTL         Connects to Analog PCB
     J5         10    JTAG
     J6          2    Reset
     J7          2    Switch
     J8          2    PD
     J9         10    LED
    J10         10    RS232
    J11         10    ToBRC

    The following pinout for lasers using a HD15 head connector was posted on Photonlexicon.

    Controller laser head connector (HD15F) pinout: 

      Pin    Wire Color   Function
 -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
   1     Light green  Laser Diode Anode (+)
   2     Blue         Laser Diode Cathode (-)
   3     ???          NC
   4     ???          NC
   5     Red          Beam Pickoff Photodiode
   6     Gray         Laser Diode Anode (+)
   7     Orange       Laser Diode Cathode (-)
   8     Dark Green   Laser Diode TEC(+)
   9     Yellow       Laser Diode TEC(-)
  10     Black        Beam Pickoff Photodiode
  11     Pink         Crystal Assembly TEC (-) *
  12     Black        Crystal Assembly Thermistor
  13     Purple       Crystal Assembly TEC (+) *
  14     Brown        Thermistor Common
  15     Red          Laser Diode Thermistor

    * On some versions, these two wires may be swapped.


  • Back to Commercial Solid State Lasers Sub-Table of Contents.

    Laser Export Model LCM-DTL-374QT Q-switched UV DPSS Laser

    Laser Export is a Russian company that manufactures some fairly sophisticated DPSS lasers. The LCM-DTL-374QT is an actively Q-switched frequency tripled diode pumped Nd:YAG laser with an average pulse energy of 20 uJ, a pulse width of 3 to 7 ns, and a repetition rate of 200 Hz to 1 kHz. They have been showing up on eBay dirt cheap, many of them actually working. It consists of a compact controller and laser head attached by a 15 pin umbilical and separate RF cable for the Q-switch as shown in Laser Export Model ACM-DTL-374AT DPSS Laser System.

    Interior View of Laser Export Model ACM-DTL-374AT DPSS Laser Head shows the arrangement of the optical components. From left to right are the dual pump diodes combined in a polarizing beam splitter, AO Q-switch, SHG (Second Harmonic (Generator) and SFM (Sum Frequency Mixing) non-linear crystals, and output optics.

    Interior View of Laser Export Model LCM-DTL-374QT Laser Controller shows the main PCB. The Q-switch driver is a self-contained module mounter underneath.

    The laser is powered from 24 VDC and will free-run at 200 Hz but also accepts a Q-switch trigger input up to 1 kHz. It's not clear why the repetition rate is limited to 1 kHz. A Nd:YAG Q-switched laser using a CW pump source should run at repetition rates up to 4 or 5 kHz without any significant drop in pulse energy and linearly increasing average power. But possibly this is limited by the Q-switch driver or power supply.

    The LCM-DTL-374QT I have has a power supply problem. With the nominal 24 VDC power input, it flashes the error code for "power out of range". At a bit over 27 VDC (yes I risked it!), this error goes away and it starts flashing the "thermal stabilizing" code, but never actually draws any significant current or comes ready and never turns on the pump diodes. So, some independent circuit may think the voltage is too high, or there are a combination of problems. The laser head is almost certainly in good condition, as is most of the power supply and the AO Q-switch driver module. Although Laser Export is based in Moscow, most or all of the electronic components are common types of Western manufacture and readily identifiable. However, many of them are surface mount parts with some underneath the main PCB so troubleshooting, especially without a schematic, would be a challenge. The problem is probably a single blown 5 cent part due to a power spike! This laser is in excellent physical condition and rather well made, though it seems Russians need to discover the benefits of hex head or Philips head screws as most of the internal fasteners are flat head! But can you believe it, there aren't even any vacuum tubes or magnetic relays for logic inside! :)

    If I can find a similar laser with a bad laser head but good controller, it should be possible to do a swap as there is only 1 pot in the controller, so setting that in the good controller to the same resistance value as tho one in the bad controller should get close. I don't believe there are any microprocessors or EEPROMS in the controller.

    I have also considered attaching the laser head to a lab controller. But even determining the laser head connectors will take some effort since there is some circuitry inside, not just wires. But I think I've identified the TEC and LD connections (apparently only one pair of wires for both LDs - current sharing must be taken care of in the laser head). But the temperature sensor is buried and the light sensors go through pots in the laser head so identifying their connection will take more work.

    If anyone has additional technical service information on these lasers (I have what passes for the user manual) or one they'd like to sell or trade, please contact me via the Sci.Electronics.Repair FAQ Email Links Page.


  • Back to Commercial Solid State Lasers Sub-Table of Contents.

    Crystalaser DPSS Lasers

    Crystalaser is a Far East manufacturer of diode and DPSS lasers. The latter appear to be based on their own optically contacted or optical bonded composite crystals or Multi-Crystal Assemblies (MCAs), although a few use an L-fold discrete cavity. They use a C-mount 1 or 2 watt pump diode with both fast and slow axis beam correction (lenses and an anamorphic prism pair), followed by a focusing lens. A beam expander/collimator is placed at the output. However, neither of the two versions I've seen had an IR-blocking filter and one of these was supposedly un-modified so that's a bit strange.

    I've seen several samples of Crystalaser DPSS lasers including a GCL-25-S laserhead, and complete GCL-150-S and CGL-200-S systems. The "S" means Single Longitudinal Mode (SLM), which would make it suitable for holography and other similar applications.

    The GCL-25-S laser head had a physically broken LD TEC and was missing the output optics entirely. The pump diode tested good, so repair might be possible in the future. The GCL-150-S system had supposedly been bought new by a company that then sold it as "surplus to their needs". But it has some serious stability problems. In particular, it refused to produce any decent power and would run multi-spatial mode. Careful adjustment of the MCA position could get it to produce a TEM00, which it would retain until power was turned off. It would also have decent, thought not perfect SLM - maybe 90 percent with at least one small additional mode. However, if power was turned off for awhile and the laser was then restarted, it might behave for awhile, but then would suddently go back to multi-spatial mode and low power. Adjustment of the pump diode and crystal temperature could affect the SLM purity from totally multimode to nearly pure SLM. But this didn't seem to have any effect on the stability issues. I do not know what could be wrong other than something with the MCA, but even then can't really speculate on what it might be that wouldn't have much more permanent effects.

    Some laser heads include an angled beam sampler plate reflecting a portion of the beam to a photodiode for output power monitoring and/or control.

    Here are some photos of the GCL-150-S, but the GCL-25-S would be virtually identical. The OEM controller PCB is similar but substitutes a ribbon cable header for the DB9 and a small 2 pin header for power, 12 VDC.

    • Crystalaser CGL-150-S System. Crystalaser seems to be fond of that rainbow ribbon cable! :)

    • Interior of Crystalaser CL-2000 Controller.
      • The light blue pot to the far left is for crystal temperature.

      • The light blue pot near it behind the power transistor is for LD temperature.

      • The gray trimpot accessible from the rear panel is for output power. It's considered a user adjustment but heck, I'd rather have a pot on the front panel. :) On the OEM version of the controller PCB, it faces up so the cover must be removed to adjust it. The hole still exists though. :)

      • The single turn trimpot in front of the RJ11 interlock connector appears to be a user current limit.

      The other two gray trimpots near the left side of the case are temperature adjustments for the cavity (left) and diode (right).

      The only interlock is a jumper between the two middle pins of the RJ11.

    • Interior of Crystalaser CGL-150-S Laser Head. From left to right are the C-mount pump diode, pump beam expander/collimator, anamorphic prism pair, pump focusing lens, Multiple Crystal Assembly (MCA), and output optics.

    • Closeup of Crystalaser CGL-150-S Multiple Crystal Assembly. That little square stuck in the middle of the large plate is the vanadate. The plate is probably sapphire for heat removal and KTP is hidden inside the metal block.

    The CGL-200-S is interesting is that (1) it uses a miniature discrete L-fold cavity configuration rather than a composite crystal assembly most commonly found in Crystalaser laser heads and (2) it is much newer than the others with a manufacturing date of 2006 versus 2000-2002. So, the discrete cavity must have been an "improvement" required for the higher output power (200 mW). The L-fold is more efficient in capturing the frequency-doubled green output since both the forward and backward traveling beams are virtually totally passed to the output. The CGL-200-S uses the same controller as the others. However, it does NOT inherently force SLM.

    Here are photos of the CGL-200S laser head:

    • Interior of Crystalaser CGL-200-S Laser Head. From left to right are the C-mount pump diode, pump beam expander/collimator, anamorphic prism pair, pump focusing lens, discrete L-Fold cavity protected with glass plates), and output optics which consist of a beam expander with IR-blocking filter. The photodiode for output power monitoring and light feedback control can be seen next to the the L-fold cavity.

    • Closeup of Crystalaser CGL-200-S Discrete L-Fold Cavity Assembly. At the left toward the front is the wedged Vanadate crystal which has a planar HR coated on its rear surface, with the L-fold HR mirror (HR@1064nm,HR@532 nm) next to it. The angled KTP crystal can be see inside the aluminum "U" box with the "OC" mirror (HR@1064nm,HT@532nm) at the upper right. (There is nothing between the KTP and OC; what appears to be a tiny glass cylinder is an optical illusion resulting from the way the aluminum block was machined.) The wedge-prism at the upper right corrects for the astigmatism introduced by the wedged Vanadate as well as adjusting the direction of the beam exiting the L-fold cavity. It also acts as a beam sampler for the beam monitoring photodiode visible at the bottom of the photo.

    The RCL-150-671-S also uses an L-fold cavity but it is about twice the length of the one for the CGL-200-S. These photos show it powered.

    • Interior of Crystalaser RCL-150-671-S Laser Head. From left to right are the C-mount pump diode, pump beam expander/collimator, anamorphic prism pair, pump focusing lens, discrete L-Fold cavity protected with glass plates), and output optics.

    • Closeup of Crystalaser RCL-150-671-S Discrete L-Fold Cavity Assembly. At the left toward the front is the wedged Vanadate crystal which has a planar HR coated on its rear surface, with the L-fold HR mirror (HR@1342nm,HR@671 nm) next to it. The long angled SHG crystal can be see inside the aluminum "U" box with the "OC" mirror (HR@1342nm,HT@671nm) at the upper right. The type of SHG crystal is not known but may be BBO.

    All of these would be very easy lasers to drive with a home-built or third party controller like the LDC 3900 due to their straightforward design and simple wiring. Here is the pinout for the laser head: 

      Pin   Color             Function
----------------------------------------------
   1   Red                LD TEC+
   2   Yellow             LD- (Cathode)
   3   Green or Blue (1)  TEC- Common
   4   Gray               Crystal thermistor
   5   White              Crystal TEC+
   6   Brown              LD thermistor
   7   Orange             LD+ (Anode, case)
   8   Violet             LD thermistor (2)
   9   Black              Crystal thermistor

    Notes:

    1. The green and blue wires are soldered to pin 3 but may go to either the LD or Crystal TEC, apparently at random.

    2. In laser heads with a beam sampler plate for output power monitoring or control, the violet wire first goes to a photodiode (PDC?) in parallel with a 100 ohm resistor, then (PDA?) to the LD thermistor. This does not affect operation when in constant current mode and either laser head will work with the CL-2000 controller (in constant current mode at least).

    I've only seen three samples so am not really sure if the pinouts and/or wire colors are the same for all. But so far, they appear to be consistently inconsistent, though compatible. :)

    LASOS DPSS Lasers

    LASOS (who also makes HeNe lasers among others) has a line of small DPSS lasers at a variety of wavelengths including 473 nm, 532 nm, 540 nm, 561 nm, and 639 nm. They all consist of a compact laser head and separate controller. General Information may be found on the LASOS Web site. These are all single mode/single frequency with a spec'd line-width of less than 5 MHz making them ideal for holography and interferometry.

    LASOS Nova50 532 nm DPSS Lasers

    The lasers I've tested were all LASOS Nova50s (which may also be named LasNova50), 532 nm with a nominal output power of 50 mW and single frequency (single longitudinal mode). (It is not known if the "50" is at all correlated with the output power!) The OEM CKL2400 controller is spec'd to work using either 12 VDC or 24 VDC, though I don't know if it will be happy in between. Lasing parameters are stored in the laser head, so controllers are totally interchangeable. When power is applied, the controller goes through thermal stabilization before ramping up the output power to the spec'd power over a total of around 5 minutes. The top LED on the controller next to the laser head cable connector is for POWER ON. The one below is mostly off but flashes somewhat randomly during the first part of initialization, mostly before the beam appears. The following is (very roughly) for one healthy sample:

    • Minute 1: Nothing, except for the flashing LED.
    • Minute 2: The beam appears.
    • Minutes 3 and 4: Output power increases to a few mW.
    • Minute 5: Output power ramps up to ~50 mW.

    The spec says it may take up to ten minutes. The output from a healthy specimen will be pure SLM almost all the time, even during warmup. Very soon after the output power has reached the spec'd value, everything will be very stable.

    Where a laser head is unable to reach the spec'd output power, it will simply max out with no light feedback running (presumably) at the diode current limit. In these cases, the power will vary over time due to slight changes in temperature. However, with one head that must have been marginal, the power reached around 50 mW, but the TEC controller seemed to be doing a dance whereby the lasing mode moved back and forth by around 1 GHz with a 1 or 2 second cycle, along with a slight change in output power. Other below-spec heads included one at around 40 mW max which was SLM most of the time and another at around 7 mW max which was not. If it were possible to change the power setting, that 40 mW head would probably be usable at 25 mW or less. And if cooling is marginal - either external or due to a problem with the TEC(s) - or the stored parameters are incorrect, the power may decline as the pump current is increased above a certain point, in which case the stupid controller will just keep increasing it until the current limit is reached and sit there. One such head peaked at around 45 mW and then declined to 13 mW. The 7 mW and 13 mW heads would make nice paperweights. :)

    There is an RS232 port on the CKL-2400 controller. A Windows program called "LASOS Commander" is able to talk to a variety of LASOS lasers. It can display the output power, pump diode current, pump diode and resonator temperatures, as well as the operating hours and serial number, and can turn the laser on and off. But "Off" here is similar to powering down as it goes through the normal 5 to 10 minute warmup when turned "On" again. The set-point output power can be changed on the "Basic View" screen, which is what comes up as default. There are also "Power", "Current", "Temperature", and "Setup" screens under "Expert View". The Setup screen has the capability of saving 3 Preset output power levels as well as the default when powered up.

    It doesn't appear possible to change anything else. Perhaps it has a "Factory Mode" but no documentation on how to access it. There has also been some mention that what is changeable depends on the specific model variation of the laser head. A Web search will find documentation on LASOS Commander but it doesn't appear on the LASOS Web site (though information on the LASOS DPSS lasers is readily available there). I can provide a copy upon request. It runs on Windows 2000, XP, and 7 through 11, don't know about Windows 3.1. ;-) However, the version I have (V1.2) is later than what's in the manual found via a Web search (V0.9). There are some subtle differences (mostly style).

    All the LASOS controllers have RS232 and/or USB. The RS232 settings are: 19,200 baud, No parity, 8 data bits, 1 stop bit, pins 2/3 direct (NOT swapped). When powered on using an ancient video display terminal or terminal emulator (e.g., Procomm for Windows), it displays a line of almost decipherable gibberish which I assume includes version number and hours and stuff like that but it's not totally obvious. So assume that as a practical matter, LASOS Commander must be used, for what it's worth.

    LASOS DPSS laser head cable wiring

    Since one of the lasers had its umbilical deliberately severed with the controller-end missing, I had to make my own cable after comparison with an intact one: 

      AMP 24  DB15M  Wire Color
-------------------------------
    1       3    Brown
    2       3    Green
    3       6    Yellow/Brown
    4       6    Pink/Brown
    5       8    Pink
    6       1    Green/Brown
    7      15    Violet
    8       9    Green/Brown
    9      11    Pink/Gray
   10       2    Blue/Red
   11      NC
   12      10    Blue
   13      NC
   14      12    Red
   15      NC
   16      13    White/Gray
   17      Index pin
   18      NC
   19      9     Gray
   20      14    Black
   21      5     Yellow/White
   22      5     Yellow/Pink
   23      4     White
   24      4     Yellow

    My pin numbering of the AMP connector is based on normal twin row headers, alternating between rows starting at label side and label end. I do not know if this is correct since there is no marking for pin 1. My cable is ugly but works.

    LASOS Nova50 Fiber-Coupled LASER

    There are also fiber-coupled versions of these lasers using polarization-maintaining single or multi-mode fibers. The only thing noteworthy about them is that with the fiber coupler removed, the laser itself produces much more output power than the free-space version. The sample I tested defaults to 100 mW free-space but has the same LASNOVA 50 label on the laser head. (It may be that the "50" has nothing to do with power.) Whether it has a beefier pump diode or is just a normal one running at higher current is not known but warmup behavior is similar. Without the fiber coupler, the beam has a divergence on the order of 4 or 5 mR but a 6 inch focal length lens reduces that to between 1 and 2 mR, which is probably near diffraction limited for the narrow beam. With the fiber coupler removed, a 1/2" diameter lens easily slips into the recess on the front of the head. It can either be secured in place with a bead of 5 Minute Epoxy or RTV Silicone around its edge, or two screws and washers in existing holes. Even if the raw divergence is acceptable, adding something to seal the hole is recommended to protect the internal optics against contamination. They would be virtually impossible to clean.


  • Back to Commercial Solid State Lasers Sub-Table of Contents.

    The Transverse Green DPSS Laser

    These are among the least expensive medium power green DPSS lasers available. However, the low cost (under $1,000) comes at a price (no pun...). The heart of the laser - the pump diode and vanadate - are glued together making any repair or modifications difficult if not impossible. More fundamentally, there is no beam shaping of the pump diode output so the output beam may not have a nice circular Gaussian profile even if it is TEM00. See: Transverse Industries, Ltd. for links to complete specifications of their TIM622 series of green DPSS lasers (as well as other assorted gadgetry).

    Older versions have no temperature control other than 1 or 2 fans. With these, power output is extremely sensitive to ambient temperature and stability may not even be on par with a typical green laser pointer. I have one sample that varies literally by 3:1 in output power between 68 and 78 °F (20 to 25 °C). I don't know if that is typical but I don't believe such a wide variatons is considered defective. It's hard to believe that one can sell something so finicky but I guess most people don't have a laser power meter to check it. The temperature range over which it reaches spec'd output power is probably much less than 1 °C. Not surprisingly, I think that Transverse has since added temperature regulation. On the plus side, this unit has a very nice beam profile - possibly better than that of a $38,000 Coherent 532.

    Thanks to Chay Donohoe for reverse engineering the TIM622 power supply. Since Transverse is one of those companies that sands off the chip numbers, he had to sacrifice one unit to actually crack open the ICs to see the part number on the die! There is certainly nothing special to warrant such paranoia. See TIM622 DPSS Laser Power Supply. It's just a constant current driver with very little to write home about. :) So what's the point of trying to keep it a secret!

    In an effort to improve the stability, I am add a circuit to control the main fan based on the temperature of the DPSS module. See the section: Simple Temperature Controlled Fan. This greatly reduces power fluctuations after warmup.

    I consider the laser described in the sections starting with: Reconstruction of an 80 mW Green DPSSFD Laser to be of a more sophisticated design, even if it does have all sorts of quality and manufacturing problems.

    Newer versions of this laser have (possibly as an option) the addition of a temperature display and pots to control fan speed. Woopie. :)

    (From: Mazz (mazz@ntlworld.com).)

    I've just bought a 50 mW DPSS laser from Transverse. It cost me $800 which was a bit more than the original price of $600, but they'd "modified the cooling" since the original quote I got.

    Anyway, there are still 2 fans on them, one extract and one blower onto the main heatsink. The mod consists of an LCD display showing the temperature of the heatsink and details of the optimum temperature at which it should be run. Each fan is connected to a potentiometer to vary its speed. This is very crude temp control and would need constant adjustment to attain optimum operating temperature. It's not really worth the extra $200 or so though apparently the laser diode supply/regulator is improved as it ramps up to running voltage!!

    I've since added a thermistor and simple feedback circuit which keeps my temperature regulated to the optimum 32 °C.

    (From: Mike Poulton (tjpoulton@aol.com).)

    I just took delivery of my newest laser - a 50 mW 532 nm DPSS module, Transverse model TIM622. They have several models available at other power ratings. I got it from a guy named Scott Smith, of PWS in Fresno, CA (scott93727@aol.com). He imports them from Transverse Technologies in Taiwan. These are not the highest quality lasers in the world, but they are a very good deal at $995. The rated specs are: 50 mW, 532 nm, less than 2 mR divergence, 100:1 polarization, TEM00, and 2,000 hour life. That last one is what gets me - they should last a lot longer than 2,000 hours. However, they are warranted for 2,000, so they will probably go for awhile after that. It came with complete test data sheets, indicating that it greatly exceeds these specification - 70 mW, 0.3 mR divergence. The pump diode is rated 2 W and is being run at 1.1 W, so it really should keep going for awhile. The beam profile is a bit sketchy - it's sort of a slightly skewed Gaussian, but hey - it's pretty good, and it should do holography with no problem. I don't think you'll find 50+ mW of green light (especially not at such high apparent intensity - 532 is really bright), guaranteed for at least 2 K hours, for less than that price anywhere. Most argon tubes won't do more than 5,000 hours or so, and they degrade over time, weigh a lot, and require massive amounts of power. This thing is likely to keep above 50 mW until the bitter end, it uses less than 24 W of input, and it weighs about a pound. Another big advantage is the small size (3.5" x 4" x 5.75") and 12 VDC power. I love it!

    Note that I'ms not affiliated with his company in any way, and I have no long-term experience with these lasers - I'm just real happy I got something this bright for about a thousand bucks!

    (From: Lynn Strickland (stricks760@earthlink.net).)

    How's the power stability over time? How repeatable is the power at turn-on? (Does it come on at 70 mW sometimes, 55 mW sometimes, etc.)?

    Any idea of the percent optical noise (i.e., does it have the green noise problem?).

    Lifetime limiting factor is probably the pump diode. How does he guarantee 2,000 hours, when (I assume) the thing doesn't have an hour-meter?

The pump diode is rated 2 A and is being run at 1.16 A, if the spec sheet is real (it's from the diode manufacturer, not Transverse). I wondered the same thing you are, though: How do you guarantee lifetime if it has no hour meter?

This is an insurance-funded replacement for a 25 mW, 488 nm argon laser that was damaged in an incident involving toxic mold (long story, don't ask). That argon had 2,500 hours on it but was still doing fine until the microbes hit. This DPSS unit (which is sitting beside me right now, illuminating the wall) is far more fun. I essentially got it for $600, since that's what I paid for the argon to begin with. It's real hard to believe this is a lemon, but I guess I'll find out in a couple months. If it's the pump diode that goes out, I may be able to replace it with a 1.2 W fiber-coupled unit I have and a GRIN lens. If all else fails, it sure is a neat little fan-cooled case! Oh, well.

    (From: Bob.)
