    (From: Joachim Mueller (JoachimMueller@swol.de).)

    It is not possible to remove the vanadate-chip because it is glued to the diode I had 2 of the units from a friend with dead diodes and he wanted me to repair them. There is no way to that! You will definitely break the chip or damage the coatings. Maybe you are lucky and the thing runs long time. Because they drive the diode with only half of the power, lifetime should be longer.

    If you want to see beam characteristics, unscrew the collimator lens at the front. Then you have a large spot and can see if it is a Gaussian shape. You don't need to measure noise. It is clear that such a laser (and nearly all low-price DPSS) have more or less noise and also large power fluctuations (10 to 20%). But this is not important for a show device.

    Maybe you can put an hour-meter to the thing and later let us all know, what lifetime it reached (hopefully more than 1,000 hours).

    (From: Hays Goodman (HGood501@aol.com).)

    This is my first laser, so I thought I'd relate briefly my experiences with the Transverse DPSS laser.

    I've probably operated my 60 mW model a total of around twenty hours or so, and so far I am extremely pleased with the performance. Warm-up time is heavily temperature-dependent. I received the laser in the dead of winter, and it was often reluctant to fire up until as much as a twenty-minute warm up time, then the beam would stabilize. Now that summer is here and indoor ambient temperatures have probably risen by fifteen degrees or so, warmup is in the several minute range before the beam really settles down. Power also seems greater (I don't own a power meter), but it's definitely brighter at 75 °F ambient as opposed to 60 °F ambient.

    On a purely aesthetic level, the beam is lovely. Solid and extremely bright, even when reflected and somewhat diffused. With a light layer of smoke in the air, even large "cones" are spectacularly visible. With the beam projected in open space, going out a thousand feet or so produces a beam about closed-fist diameter; I intend to experiment with external collimation to see if this can be improved. The beam visibility definitely makes safety a bit less onerous, since beam paths are easily detected in low-light conditions. Best of all it's light, small, easily transported and quiet, the dual fans making only a slight sound like a computer tower.

    I can't speak to lifetime yet, but to this point it's been a dream.


  • Back to Commercial Solid State Lasers Sub-Table of Contents.

    Changehun New Industries DPSS Lasers

    Changehun New Industries (CNI) is a Chinese manufacturer of a wide variety of DPSS lasers in almost every wavelength imaginable from 266 to 2,200 nm. from 1 mW to 10 WATTs, CW (including low noise), passively and actively Q-switched, and other optioncs with either lab or OEM controllers.

    Changehun New Industries MGL-III-532/100 Green (532 nm) DPSS Laser

    The first laser I tested was an MGL-III-532/300 with the PSU-III-OEM controller as shown in CNI MGL-III/300 DPSS Laser System. It's specifications can be found at CNI MGL-III-532 Datasheet.

    A photo of the inside of the very similar 543 nm model is shown in CNI MGL-III-543/100 Green (543 nm) DPSS Laser Head Interior (and described in the next section). (The only significant physical difference is that the 532 nm version lacks the light feedback components.) The construction is so remarkably similar to that of the laser at the heart of the saga describe at Reconstruction of an 80 mW Green DPSSFD Laser that I am forced to conclude that CNI (or its predecessor in 2000, 12 years ago!) must have been the manufacturer of that one as well. Or, at least that unfortunately, CNI copied from them. There have been some improvements such as the use of a standard laser diode (similar to C-mount) instead of a bare chip, and there are no anamorphic prisms for pump beam shaping, only a single lens. But the way the cavity is put together is very similar.

    Among its construction "features" are:

  • The 3x3x1mm Nd:YVO4 crystal is sandwiched between a slot in the aluminum resonator frame and a copper plate with a beveled hole for the pump beam. Additional copper pieces fill the remainder of the slot, presumably to help with heat dissipation. But there is no indium foil or other material either for improving heat conductivity or protecting the fragile crystal. So, the 4 screws must be tightened evenly and just enough to hold the crystal snug without cracking it since the slot is just slightly less deep than the thickness of the crystal.

  • The OC mirror is mounted in an aluminum plate at the other end of the resonator secured with 3 screws. The 4th was missing.

  • The KTP crystal is inside a copper block secured with 2 screws, flat head in this case. (All the others are Philips.)

  • The TEC for the KTP is glued to the resonator frame, and thus controls the temperature of the entire resonator assembly. But it has no indium foil or heat-sink grease to improve thermal conductivity to the case.

  • The resonator is covered with a thin plastic box affair, presumably to provide some thermal isolation. It is not sealed and probably not very effective.

  • The wiring from the head cable to the internal components is spliced and covered with red or black heat-shrink tubing, not all of which was shrunk completely, so some pieces slid off of the splices. (Or perhaps it was simply tubing with no "shrink".)

  • The beam expanding output optic is glued to the wall of the head case and seals that hole. The collimating lens and IR-blocking filter are in a removable assembly. Even with the filter in place, output power reads around 25 percent high due to IR leakage.

    The OEM controller is mounted on a fan-cooled heat-sink as shown in CNI MGL-III/300 DPSS Laser OEM Controller. and runs on 5 VDC. It is mated to the specific laser head via a cable hardwired at the laser head with a DB9 connector at the controller. It has 4 trim-pots. Those for the LD and RES/KTP TECs have been identified:

    • The LD TEC trim-pot is the left-rear one in the photo.
    • The RES/KTP TEC trim-pot is the right-front one in the photo.

    TEC response is relatively sluggish, possibly due to limited drive capability from their drivers.

    This particular laser has an analog modulation input, supposedly with a range of 0 (no output) to 5 V (maximum output). However, maximum output actually occurs at somewhat less than 5 V, with the output actually above this point.

    Changehun New Industries MGL-III-543/100 Green (543 nm) DPSS Laser

    I didn't even realize there was a DPSS line that could be doubled to 543 nm, nearly matching the wavelength of the green HeNe laser. But there is a 1,086 nm Nd:YVO4 line, so with narrow-band mirror coatings, it's possible to double that to 543 nm, most likely using Type II phase matched KTP, similar to 532 nm. The interior construction of the 543 nm laser shown in CNI MGL-III-543/100 Green (543 nm) DPSS Laser Head Interior is virtually the same as the 532 nm version, though the 543 nm laser has a beam sampler and photodiode for light feedback, lacking on the 532 nm laser. It is mated to another version of the MGL-III controller, but with an HD15 connector on the cable, presumably because the photodiode signals required an extra pin or two. But the controller is otherwise essentially identical, but apparently without the modulation capability of the 532 nm version.

    This laser was originally outputting 1 to 2 mW, just a wee bit less than its 100 mW output power specification. :( The beam - what of it that was present - was relatively circular but splotchy. This suggested that one or more of the intra-cavity optic surfaces had degraded or been damaged. If the pump diode has simply become weak, the beam profile should still have been relatively smooth and Gaussian. If alignment were the issue, the beam would have appeared multi-spatial mode in some way. And adjusting the pump focus lens and/or KTP orientation resulted in *minimum* power for the apparent optimal position. To make long story slightly shorter, The Vanadate crystal was removed and reinstalled, and alignment of the pump focus lens, KTP, and overall cavity were performed iteratively resulting in a whopping 25 mW after a short 15 to 20 minute warmup. I had seen over 30 mW but couldn't get back to that for some reason. In order to even get the 25 mW required raising the entire cavity with a shim plate since the optimal pump beam position on the Vanadate had changed. The normal degrees of freedom for alignment are extremely limited, so I consider a bit of a miracle even getting this far. Whether this will survive for any length of time or with any sort of even normal handling is not known. But it has been repeatable over several power cycles. ;-)

    More to come.

    Calmar DPSS Mode-Locked Femtosecond Fiber Laser

    This is a rather unusual laser to be sitting along side the others in this chapter but I couldn't think of anywhere else to put it. :) Calmar Optcom, Inc. manufactures a variety of ultra-fast fiber lasers. The gain medium in a fiber laser is, well, a fiber. :) Specifically in this case, an erbium doped fiber, similar to the Erbium Doped Fiber Amplifiers (EDFAs) that make possible trans-oceanic and other modern forms of long distance communication and the Internet.

    The laser I had was some version of a FPL-01T, no longer a current product. In fact, from the internal construction, this may have been a prototype. Screws were missing, holes were drilled out of line, and the general quality level seemed closer to that of a science fair project than production laser. However, some info can be found at Calmar FPL-01 and FPL-01T Ultrafast Er-Fiber Lasers. It is a tunable passively mode-locked laser with a sub-nanosecond pulse width and pulse repetition rate of 5 to 100 MHz (depending on version, dependant on the total length of the fiber cavity). In a nutshell, a fiber-coupled laser diode (probably around 980 nm) is the pump source which feeds a stretch of erbium-doped fiber which is part of the fiber laser cavity. At one end is fiber coupler to convert the output of the fiber to a collimated beam, followed by a saturable absorber and mirror; at the other end is a fiber coupler aimed at a diffraction grating on an adjustable mount. The total length of the cavity determines the pulse rate, which in the case of this laser should be about 25 MHz for a total length of roughly 27 feet assuming an index of refraction for the fiber of 1.5.

    When I received this laser it did exactly nothing. Someone or someones had been inside and had apparently twiddled several of the adjustments that were not meant to be twiddled. ;-) See Interior of Calmar FPL-01T Mode-Locked Fiber Laser. At the top of the photo is the diffraction grating on a New Focus kinematic mount to the left of the coupler for the white fiber. At the bottom is the saturable absorber and mirror attached to the brass plate and the coupler for the white fiber. All the magic happens behind the white and yellow foam, which I did not intend to disturb if possible.

    There are both direct (split off the main cavity) and amplified outputs (PM FC/APC connectors) on the front panel, as well as an electrical output on the rear panel from a high speed photodiode. Needless to say, I have no means of viewing sub-picosecond pulses even if there were some to view. However, from past experience, a 100 MHz scope will be able to detect short pulses even if not accurately represented.

    So, my mission was to get this laser going flying almost blind. :( ;-) Calmar was particularly unhelpful in this regard. Their main concern seemed to be to sell the owner a replacement at $10,000 or more. The best that they could offer was that most relevant information was Company proprietary and offered that "the laser is tuned using the tuning knob and you can monitor the output on the rear panel". (I've paraphrased only slightly.) So, tell me something I don't know! :) Some companies will be more than willing to assist knowing it will create good will and possible future sales, even if they lose the repair business. So be it. This isn't rocket science or even really convoluted laser science. It's just a very long laser cavity. What could be so hard about that? :)

    My initial attempts were discouraging. Looking at the photo, there is a scribe line visible around the base of the white fiber coupler. No one I contacted who may have been the fiddlers :) could say for sure who put that there. I attached the fiber from a red HeNe to that coupler and found that its output didn't seem to hit the grating at all, no matter the setting of the tuning adjustment. So, perhaps, that scribe line should be ignored! In addition, that New Focus mount has two other screws in addition to the tuning knob. Supposedly, they hadn't been touched but.... Of course, there was no lasing evident via either front panel fiber output.

    Fortunately, that electrical monitor connector on the real panel is all that's needed for alignment. Even with no lasing, there is a DC level there probably from the fluorescence (spontaneous emission) in the fiber. And that is effected by alignment of the yellow fiber collimator. Presumably, what's happening is that whatever gets reflected back into the cavity adds to the light in the fiber and the detected DC level. I had been told that 2 screws were missing from the yellow fiber collimator assembly and they had been replaced but the alignment was lost. Really? Those screws were used for alignment and one doesn't simply tighten screws that appear loose! ;-) So the first step was to adjust the alignment of the yellow fiber collimator so that the reflection from the saturable absorber/mirror was maximum. This requires that both the distance from the fiber tip to the collimating lens and orientation be optimized. There are 6 screws on the collimator - 3 to adjust it and 3 to lock it. More or less. The coarse distance to the saturable absorber can also be changed by loosening the screws securing the collimator assembly to the baseplate but if as is assumed, the output is collimated, this really shouldn't matter. There are also 3 screws on the back of the brass plate which affect alignment.

    Even having been messed up, adjustment of the 3 most relevant screws did have an effect and peaking that alone would have probably been sufficient to optimize alignment there. However, the coupler was at a steep angle and I was hoping to reduce that while also enabling everything to be tightened down. So, I attempted to adjust the assembly on the baseplate and for awhile, lost it entirely. But with a bit of additional fiddling was able to get back to where I started and also improve the alignment to the point where it probably was as good as it was going to get. And all the screws were at least snug sot they probably wouldn't change on their own.

    Next up: The grating. No adjustment of the micrometer tuning knob, or even moving that entire assembly by loosening the mounting screws had any effect. Not wanting to change the settings of the other adjustments just yet, I figured that if a grating is supposed to work as a feedback "mirror", then a real planar mirror should work as well. So, as a quick test, I positioned an aluminum first surface mirror in front of the coupler and indeed, was able to get a very dramatic change in the monitored signal without really trying. Thus, the conclusion was that the grating adjustment was way off. Pulling the front panel to gain access to the other 2 screws of the New Focus mount would enable the alignment to be found by an exhaustive search. With the mounting screws loose enough to enable the mount to be moved from side-to-side, the Y adjustment screw was turned incrementally. Indeed, after only a couple minutes, a change could be seen in the electrical output. Then, with the mount locked down, the Y adjustment and tuning knob could be used to reliably peak the output. Which now consists of pulses at about 25 MHz. The tuning range is about 1 turn of the micrometer knob. Checking the front panel fiber outputs using a Newport high speed photodiode preamp, both optical signals are now there with a much stronger signal from the amplified output.

    I have no way of knowing if the pulses are really sub-picosecond but for a passively mode-locked laser, it's likely that they will either meet spec or wont be there at all. The range of diode current over which this lases is somewhat limited - from about 175 to the maximum 202 (mA I assume), so it's possible more optimization is needed, or the diode is somewhat weak.

    I now suspect that the white fiber collimator in the tuning assembly must have been disassembled and put back together incorrectly rotated by +90 or -90 degrees so that its beam is not parallel to the base, thus necessitating significant Y tilt of the grating mount. As caan be seen in the photo, the mount is wedged and that wedge should probably be shifting the beam up or down, not sideways. This would also limit the tuning range (as well as explaining why the scribe line is incorrect). For what the application of this particular laser tuning doesn't really matter and I've already sent the laser home. But perhaps something that can be corrected in the future.

    MPB Communications VFL Compact Visible Fiber Laser

    The VFL are a series of diode pumped frequency doubled fiber lasers that were manufactured by MPB Communications. They had output powers of up to more than 1 watt at many wavelengths spanning the visible spectrum. The design is that of a classic fiber laser - a length of double-clad optical fiber as the gain medium pumped using high power laser diodes coupled in the the gain medium cladding. The HR and OC for the fundamental are implemented as temperature controlled fiber Bragg Gratings (FBGs). A separate Second Harmonic Generator (SHG or "Doubler") box includes a temperature controlled SHG crystal (probably PPLN or something similar since the beam is unidirectional), an IR beam dump, output collimation optics, and a photodiode preamp for optical power feedback. The controller has no switches or knobs, only a pair of LEDs: SHG and Laser. All adjustments including enabling the output must be done from a computer via RS232 or USB. I do not even think it's possible to set it up to lase automatically when power is applied.

    I've seen two of these lasers. The first was a MPB VFL 592-200 rated 200 mW at 594 nm, a glorious yellow. :) Since that one worked perfectly and was capable of well over 350 mW, I didn't dig too much into it.

    The second was a VFL-P-1000-642-OEM2 rated at 1 W at 640 nm, a boring red. Unfortunately for it, but fortunate for the Laser FAQ :), the doubler head must have had a close encounter with a hard object as one of its corners was bent, and the TEC inside was physically broken. To seal its fate, someone must have gone inside not knowing how to safely remove the cover and broken the fiber. In addition, the controller to doubler box electrical cable was missing, probably salvaged for the wires. But by substituting a resistor whose value matched the temperature sensor resistance to keep the controller happy, it was possible to power up the laser. Then, using the MPB "old" control software, the laser could be turned on and adjusted via RS232. However, the pump diodes are either dead or there is some other damage as not a single IR photon could be located either inside the controller or at the broken end of the fiber even at the current limit of 7.5 A. Sad to say, but the most useful parts at this point may be the six feet, which are standard Newport posts, and the fans if one can stand the racket. :( :)

    So, it was basically junk just asking for partial dissection and photographs. ;-) (But note that nothing required for the photos would have been irreversible had the laser been undamaged.)

    • Interior of MPB VFL-P-1000-642-OEM2 Fiber Laser Controller shows what's inside the fundamental laser which is supposed to generate several watts at 1280 nm (IR) which is fed to the doubler box via an armored fiber-optic cable. A pair of multi-watt fiber-coupled pump diodes are fed into what must be 100 feet or more of the active gain fiber, all wound around the perimeter of the controller. While not everything can be identified including where joins or splits occur, the OC and HR fiber-Bragg grating locations are labeled. The OC is at the top of photo enclosed in an aluminum block that sits on a TEC with a thermistor temperature sensor. The HR is in a similar assembly, but hidden under the PCB at the left. The function of the third aluminum block near the of the photo is not known.

      The PCB in the center has the dual pump diode driver, each one capable of up to at least 4 amps. It also has the temperature controller for the OC FBG, and feeds power to the turbofans under the baseplate. There is a fan for each pump diode and a third for the driver PCB. And the term "turbo" is no exaggeration - though small in size, these are the absolute loudest fans I've heard in a laser like this.

      The PCB on the left has the USB and RS232 interfaces and remaining logic functions as well as the temperature controller for the HR FBG.

      There are no trim-pots anywhere, so all parameters are set in firmware. But it is not known how to read or change anything beyond power/current and SHG temperature.

    • Interior of MPB VFL-P-1000-642-OEM2 Fiber Laser Doubler Box shows what's inside when intact. The fiber is at the left feeding into the copper-colored block which has the SHG crystal. The output passes through a beam sampler which feeds the photodiode preamp. Prior to this is a 45 degree mirror that reflects any IR that gets past the SHG crystal into the aluminum block at the back, which is the waste beam dump. It has a geometric configuration to absorb as many of those unwanted IR photons as possible and may need to dissipate watts of IR should the SHG crystal temperature be far from optimal. Thus it is securely attached to the doubler baseplate using thermal grease to optimize contact.

    • Components of SHG Crystal Assembly in MPB VFL-P-1000-642-OEM2 Fiber Laser shows the long doubling crystal, IR-reflecting mirror, and output collimator. A top half of the broken TEC can be seen still glued to the bottom of the crystal block.

    For reference purposes: the partial doubler box connector pinout is as follows: 

      Pin   Function
 -----------------------------------------
   1    Temperature sensor 10K thermistor
   2    Temperature sensor
   3    TEC
   4    TEC
   5    PD Preamp
   6    NC
   7    NC
   8    NC
   9    PD Preamp
  10    PD Preamp

    To run the Windows Graphical User Interface (GUI) using RS232: The MPB Old Software can be used. The New Software probably works also but I have not confirmed this, only that it connects. There is no installation, just run the application. If using a USB to RS232 adapter, the GUI does NOT need to be restarted when the laser is power cycled. If direct, it probably does. To use USB, a driver must be installed, but Windows will find it if pointed to the MPB directory. The software is available free from MPB or I can provide it. However, if you are running a recent version of Windows (10 or 11), it may complain about the driver not being signed. (Older versions of Windows probably won't care.) A Web search will find a way around this. One option is to go to How to Install Unsigned Drivers on Windows 10 (minitool.com).

    Setting the value of resistance between pins 1 and 2 so the SHG Temperature readout is green on the GUI readout seems to be sufficient for the laser to turn on with nothing else attached to the SHG connector. This may need to be correct BEFORE applying DC power to the laser. I haven't seen it recover and enable the laser to turn on the output while doing adjustments when powered, though the readout does change. The SHG LED keeps flashing yellow. If close enough when powered up, the SHG LED will come on solid green and stay that way. Else it will flash yellow. Using the Windows GUI, power the laser and while monitoring the temperature (Tools: SHG Temperature), adjust the resistance between pins 1 and 2 until the readout value is near the set-point value and green. Or adjust the set-point. Then power cycle the laser. Note: None of this may work correctly for some or all OEM model MPB lasers, as yet unconfirmed.

    The laser defaults to constant power mode, which means that when turned on in the GUI, it will ramp up the current until the current limit is reached and shut down if the set output power can't be achieved. Even the fans go off. Switch to constant current mode before turning the laser on in the GUI.

    If anyone would be interested in a challenge, this unit may be available at a very reasonable price. The fiber gain medium alone should be valuable in constructing a fiber laser of your own. Sorry, I don't know its specific composition except that it operates at a fundamental wavelength of 1,280 nm. Contact me and make an offer if interested. But you don't get the feet. :-)
